mattér; wo desire, however, to state that. good

_anxous'that no sympathy with them should be 
‘allowed to interfere in any way withthe ouflow 
_Of charity to those of the Chinese Community

THE PROHIBITION OF OFITM, °: 
i 
sens an IMPERIAL EDICT. 


‘The consumption of opium is a great ‘cata. 
Mity-to the people of this’ Empire, Last year 
wo issued‘an edict prohibiting the cultivation 
aod consumption of opium. and commanded 
the Council of State to devise effective re-

The day fixed forthe closing of the opium 
‘dens in the native city of Shanghai bas passed, 
aud the bacging of fireworks in Chang Swho's 
Gardens on Saturday afternoon announced thé 
official-celebration ‘im honour of the anloree- 
ment of the prohibition, No untoward incident 
has occurred to mar the success, of the pres 

liminary step towards the total suppression 

opium-smoking in ‘Shanghai;.and for, 
satisfactory, feature the Chinese comimusily 
has to thank solely the Shanghai Tagiai. Some 
time élapacd before’ the Opium Kegulations 
ued in ao Imperial Edict last November 
were authoritatively ptomulzated, io this, 
Viceroyalty, and- conséquently the period 
of grace provided for’ the enforcement of 
their terms, wiich expired In Chihlion May 17, 
holds good for the major portion of these pro- 
vinces until August... Taotai‘ Jui, however, 
decided that ‘if the prohibition was to bs put 
into effect, the soaner the better, and fixed June 
22 a8 the day on which the opiam dens in the 
city were to close their doors, Oa‘May 27 he 
wed the first of a series of proclamation deal: | 
ing with the approaching closure of the dens, 
and -froni that date until Saturday there is 
ample evidence to | show that’ somt ‘anxiety: 
existe! in official’ minds -regarding the 
attitude of public opinian towards .: the 
measure. It required na - litle persua- 
sions and very specific threats. to’ secure 
+gennral acqiiescence in the proposed closure j. 
but liberal promises to, the employees. in the 
trade and a happy decision to throw tho re- 
sponsibility for the enforcement of the prohi- 
bition on the.gentry and public bodies brought 
-about the desired resull, Silver medals were 
to be given to the owners of deos_ who closed 
down before June 225. alltaxes were remitted 
for two months ; employees whose homes were 
in. other’ parts of the Country were.ta be repa- 
triated free of expense ; others Were to be ad- 
mitted to industrial schools where they‘ would 
have opporlunity,of learning a different trade. 


| ‘Thas-Sbanghai city bas Jallen into line with 


‘Pekitig and Tientsin, anda fresh notch can be: 
marked in the calendar of opium suppression 
in China! Haat! j a 

_ In a telegram to The Times [rom its 
Peking correspondent, which we reproduce in 
another colutin, the genesal situation a manth 
ago in regard to the opium campaiga is briefly 
set forth, ‘It is found that in only one province, 


Chibli, ore the results of the Opium Edict |" 


“wholly satisfactory ;" in six other provinces 
they. are regarded as salisfactory ina minor 
degree. To the lattec calegory may now pos: 
sibly be added this province of Kiangsu ; "but 
if we remember the relatively small. heidway 
made in the ‘serious campaign against opium, 
even wheredens have been actually ctosed, it 
is clear that China has still a very considerable 
“amount of ground. to ‘cover before she rids 
herself entirely of the evil. What has been 
accomplished in -the native city of Shanghai, 
thanks to the energy of Viceroy Tuan Kang, 
and - his vigorous « subordinate Taotai Joy is 
merely theclosure of public .resoris for the 
smoking of opium —a far less effective measure 
yowards the elimination of opium than would 
ye the. closing of public-houses: in Engtand for: 
the cause of teetotalism, No embargo has at 
present been.put-upon the sale of opium, als 
though the original scheme, which hag yet to 
be carried out in its entirety; provides for the 
licensing of retail opium shops and of smokers, 
‘There is every probability of these latter mea- 
sures being carried out wilh equal rigour 
withio’Taotai Jui's jurisdiction; but their per. 
manent enforcement is a mater of sound 
administration, which docs not lend itself to. 
‘reworks and official dispatches; atid may, 
therefore, become a dead letter. in other parts 
of the Enipire, where less capable and less 
cnergetic officials hold sway. lf wa seem to 
emphasize the little that has been actually 
accomplished as yet, (t'js not from want of 
sympathy with the antiopium movement, 
which miufsl necessarily command the tespect 
‘and admiration of every well-wisher of Chinas 
-but rather from a. desi prevent, any 
relaxation of efforts in this ‘direction on the 
part of officials and others, who may be led to 
suppose that the success of their experiment 
Non the corpus vile of a few hundred oprum 
den keepers implies the eradication—for all 
international purposcs—of the opiumevi). 

‘These considerations, however, do not Justify 
an. attempt to evade the opium issue io this 
Settlement. In all the measures: taken. by 
Taotai Julto ensure the success of the closure 
movement, the dominant idea, ‘as my. be 
gathered from one of his dispatches to Vicéroy 
‘Tuan Fang, has been to leave the forciga 
Settlement no excuse for allowing opium dens | 
within its jurisdiction, Although further official 
representations on the subject may not yet have 
reached thé Consular Body, it is clear that they: 
will arrivé sooner or later. At the same time 
public apinion at Howe will, probably lead: 
to appeals from the British Government, anda 
declaration of policy may be wrung from this 
community, which woold come with fur better 
grace if given gratuitously at once, There are 
many fealures in the situation which have to be 
borne in mind before our policy is 
declared, and. the Chinese authorities will 
he the first ‘to appreciate a aupassanae con- 
sideration of them. In-the fi 


rst. place; al- 
though the licences of opium dens are issued 
quarterly, provision has been made’.ia the 
Budget or the collection of the tax for the 


| whole financial year, and consequently the & 


are practical difficulties in'the ‘way of closing 
dawn the opium dens of the ‘Settlement before’ 
March 31, 1908. Fromthat date Chisa will,

THE flourmilling, industry is fast growing in 
Japan, as it is considere: lucrative in view. of 
the large importations of American flour. A 
marked grow.h. is noticed already. this: year.” 
For instance, the Toda flour mill, ‘the Meiji: 
fisur mill, the Teikoku flour mill and the Man- 
she flour mill concerns have been established. 
in the Kwanto district, branch mills. of the 
Masuda and Japan ‘companies have been: es- 
tablished in Kobe, while the Kobe rice clean- 
‘ing firm at Hyogo has also ptarled a flour mill. 
Some of these new mills have already com- 
menced operations, while tha others are’ to 
open shorily,, When all these mills are in fuil 
pperation, together with the old mills—the Ja- 
pan, Masuda, ‘ Nagasaki, Sapporo’ and Tate: . 
-payraht-mills=the-cutiutof—flour—in-Japan- 
willbe considerably. increased. ' A large*in- 
crease may then be seen inthe impost.of wheat 
from Australia, whil the importation of Ameri- 
can flour will be largely checked.

ol knighthood. Sir A. Hosie has been associat

province of Szechuan, bas devoted himself for
